# Agriculture

Cultivation of plants and animals

"Farming" redirects here. For other uses, see [Farming (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farming_\(disambiguation\) "Farming (disambiguation)").

This article is about agriculture for humans. For agriculture for ants, see [Agriculture in ants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_ants "Agriculture in ants").

                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |

**Agriculture** encompasses crop and [livestock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livestock "Livestock") production, [aquaculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquaculture "Aquaculture"), and [forestry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forestry "Forestry") for food and non-food products.[\[1\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-1) Agriculture was a key factor in the rise of [sedentary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sedentism "Sedentism") human [civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Civilization "Civilization"), whereby farming of [domesticated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestication "Domestication") species created food [surpluses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_surplus "Economic surplus") that enabled people to live in the cities. While humans started gathering grains at least 105,000 years ago, nascent farmers only began planting them around 11,500 years ago. Sheep, goats, pigs, and cattle were domesticated around 10,000 years ago. Plants were independently cultivated in at least 11 regions of the world. In the 20th century, [industrial agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_agriculture "Industrial agriculture") based on large-scale [monocultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monoculture "Monoculture") came to dominate agricultural output.

As of 2021[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit), [small farms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smallholding "Smallholding") produce about one-third of the world's food, but large farms are prevalent.[\[2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:2-2) The largest 1% of farms in the world are greater than 50 hectares (120 acres) and operate more than 70% of the world's farmland.[\[2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:2-2) Nearly 40% of agricultural land is found on farms larger than 1,000 hectares (2,500 acres).[\[2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:2-2) However, five of every six farms in the world consist of fewer than 2 hectares (4.9 acres), and take up only around 12% of all agricultural land.[\[2\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:2-2) Farms and farming greatly influence [rural economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rural_economics "Rural economics") and greatly shape [rural society](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rural_society "Rural society"), affecting both the direct [agricultural workforce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_workforce "Agricultural workforce") and broader [businesses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agribusiness "Agribusiness") that support the farms and farming populations.

The major agricultural products can be broadly grouped into [foods](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food "Food"), [fibers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiber "Fiber"), [fuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuel "Fuel"), and [raw materials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Raw_material "Raw material") (such as [rubber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_rubber "Natural rubber")). Food classes include [cereals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cereal "Cereal") ([grains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grains "Grains")), [vegetables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vegetable "Vegetable"), [fruits](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fruit "Fruit"), [cooking oils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooking_oil "Cooking oil"), [meat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meat "Meat"), [milk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milk "Milk"), [eggs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egg "Egg"), and [fungi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Edible_mushroom "Edible mushroom"). Global agricultural production amounts to approximately 11 billion tonnes of food,[\[3\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-3) 32 million tonnes of natural fibers[\[4\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-4) and 4 billion m3 of wood.[\[5\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-5) However, around 14% of the world's food is lost from production before reaching the retail level.[\[6\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-6)

Modern [agronomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agronomy "Agronomy"), [plant breeding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plant_breeding "Plant breeding"), [agrochemicals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agrochemical "Agrochemical") such as [pesticides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pesticide "Pesticide") and [fertilizers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertilizer "Fertilizer"), and technological developments have sharply increased [crop yields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crop_yield "Crop yield"), but also contributed to [ecological and environmental damage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_impact_of_agriculture "Environmental impact of agriculture"). [Selective breeding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selective_breeding "Selective breeding") and modern practices in [animal husbandry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_husbandry "Animal husbandry") have similarly increased the output of meat, but have raised concerns about [animal welfare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_welfare "Animal welfare") and environmental damage. Environmental issues include [contributions to climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenhouse_gas_emissions_from_agriculture "Greenhouse gas emissions from agriculture"), depletion of [aquifers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquifer "Aquifer"), [deforestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation "Deforestation"), [antibiotic resistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antibiotic_resistance "Antibiotic resistance"), and [other agricultural pollution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_pollution "Agricultural pollution"). Agriculture is both a cause of and sensitive to [environmental degradation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_degradation "Environmental degradation"), such as [biodiversity loss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity_loss "Biodiversity loss"), [desertification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desertification "Desertification"), [soil degradation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soil_degradation "Soil degradation"), and [climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change "Climate change"), all of which can cause decreases in crop yield. [Genetically modified organisms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetically_modified_organism "Genetically modified organism") are widely used, although [some countries ban them](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regulation_of_genetic_engineering "Regulation of genetic engineering").

## Etymology and scope

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=1 "Edit section: Etymology and scope")]

Further information: [Horticulture § Scope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horticulture#Scope "Horticulture")

The word *agriculture* is a late [Middle English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_English "Middle English") adaptation of Latin *agricultūra*, from *ager* 'field' and *cultūra* '[cultivation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tillage "Tillage")' or 'growing'.[\[7\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-7) While agriculture usually refers to human activities, certain species of [ant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attine_ants "Attine ants"),[\[8\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-8)[\[9\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-9) [termite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Termite "Termite") and [beetle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ambrosia_beetle "Ambrosia beetle") have been cultivating crops for up to 60 million years.[\[10\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-10) Agriculture is defined with varying scopes, in its broadest sense using natural resources to "produce commodities which maintain life, including food, fiber, forest products, horticultural crops, and their related services".[\[11\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Maine-11) Thus defined, it includes [arable farming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arable_farming "Arable farming"), horticulture, [animal husbandry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_husbandry "Animal husbandry") and [forestry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forestry "Forestry"), but horticulture and forestry are in practice often excluded.[\[11\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Maine-11) It may also be broadly decomposed into **plant agriculture**, which concerns the cultivation of useful plants,[\[12\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Stevenson1971-12) and **animal agriculture**, the production of agricultural animals.[\[13\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Herren2012-13)

## History

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=2 "Edit section: History")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/ad/Vavilov-centers_updated.jpg/300px-Vavilov-centers_updated.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Vavilov-centers_updated.jpg)

  [Centres of origin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Center_of_origin "Center of origin"), as numbered by [Nikolai Vavilov](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nikolai_Vavilov "Nikolai Vavilov") in the 1930s.

   Area 3 is no longer recognized as a center of origin

  [New Guinea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Guinea "New Guinea") (area P) was identified more recently.

[\[14\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Larson2014-14)[\[15\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-15)

Main article: [History of agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_agriculture "History of agriculture")

### Origins

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=3 "Edit section: Origins")]

Main article: [Neolithic Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic_Revolution "Neolithic Revolution")

The development of agriculture enabled the human population to grow many times larger than could be sustained by [hunting and gathering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter-gatherer "Hunter-gatherer").[\[16\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Bocquet-Appel-16) Agriculture began independently in different parts of the globe,[\[17\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Stephens_897%E2%80%93902-17) and included a diverse range of [taxa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taxa "Taxa"), in at least 11 separate [centers of origin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centers_of_origin "Centers of origin").[\[14\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Larson2014-14) Wild grains were collected and eaten from at least 105,000 years ago.[\[18\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-18) In the Paleolithic Levant, 23,000 years ago, cereals cultivation of [emmer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emmer_wheat "Emmer wheat"), [barley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barley "Barley"), and [oats](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oats "Oats") has been observed near the sea of Galilee.[\[19\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-19)[\[20\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-20) Rice was [domesticated in China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_China "Agriculture in China") between 11,500 and 6,200 BC with the earliest known cultivation from 5,700 BC,[\[21\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-21) followed by [mung](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mung_bean "Mung bean"), [soy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soy "Soy") and [azuki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azuki_bean "Azuki bean") beans. Sheep were domesticated in [Mesopotamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopotamia "Mesopotamia") between 13,000 and 11,000 years ago.[\[22\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-22) Cattle were domesticated from the wild [aurochs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aurochs "Aurochs") in the areas of modern Turkey and Pakistan some 10,500 years ago.[\[23\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-McTavish-23) [Pig production](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_pig "Domestic pig") emerged in Eurasia, including Europe, East Asia and Southwest Asia,[\[24\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-24) where [wild boar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wild_boar "Wild boar") were first domesticated about 10,500 years ago.[\[25\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-25) In the [Andes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andes "Andes") of South America, the potato was domesticated between 10,000 and 7,000 years ago, along with beans, [coca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coca "Coca"), [llamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Llama "Llama"), [alpacas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpaca "Alpaca"), and [guinea pigs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guinea_pig "Guinea pig"). [Sugarcane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugarcane "Sugarcane") and some [root vegetables](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_root_vegetables "List of root vegetables") were domesticated in [New Guinea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Guinea "New Guinea") around 9,000 years ago. [Sorghum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sorghum "Sorghum") was domesticated in the [Sahel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahel "Sahel") region of Africa by 7,000 years ago. Cotton was domesticated in Peru by 5,600 years ago,[\[26\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Broudy1979-26) and was independently domesticated in Eurasia. [In Mesoamerica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Mesoamerica "Agriculture in Mesoamerica"), wild [teosinte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Teosinte "Teosinte") was bred into [maize](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maize "Maize") (corn) from 10,000 to 6,000 years ago.[\[27\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-27)[\[28\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-benz-28)[\[29\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-29) The [horse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horse "Horse") was [domesticated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestication_of_the_horse "Domestication of the horse") in the [Eurasian Steppes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasian_Steppe "Eurasian Steppe") around 3500 BC.[\[30\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-30) Scholars have offered multiple hypotheses to explain the historical origins of agriculture. Studies of the transition from [hunter-gatherer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter-gatherer "Hunter-gatherer") to agricultural societies indicate an initial period of intensification and increasing [sedentism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sedentism "Sedentism"); examples are the [Natufian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natufian_culture "Natufian culture") in the [Levant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Levant "Levant"), and the Early Chinese Neolithic in China. Then, wild stands that had previously been harvested started to be planted, and gradually came to be domesticated.[\[31\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-31)[\[32\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-32)[\[33\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-b1-33)

### Civilizations

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=4 "Edit section: Civilizations")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e9/Centres_of_origin_and_spread_of_agriculture.svg/300px-Centres_of_origin_and_spread_of_agriculture.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Centres_of_origin_and_spread_of_agriculture.svg)

Map of the world showing approximate centers of origin of agriculture and its spread in prehistory.[\[34\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-34) DNA studies have shown that agriculture was introduced in [Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic_Europe "Neolithic Europe") by the expansion of the [early farmers from Anatolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_European_Farmers "Early European Farmers") about 9,000 years ago. [\[35\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-35)

In Eurasia, the [Sumerians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumer "Sumer") started to live in villages from about 8,000 BC, relying on the [Tigris](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tigris "Tigris") and [Euphrates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euphrates "Euphrates") rivers and a canal system for irrigation. Ploughs appear in [pictographs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pictograph "Pictograph") around 3,000 BC; seed-ploughs around 2,300 BC. Farmers grew wheat, barley, vegetables such as lentils and onions, and fruits including dates, grapes, and figs.[\[36\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-BritMus-36) [Ancient Egyptian agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egyptian_agriculture "Ancient Egyptian agriculture") relied on the [Nile River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nile_River "Nile River") and its seasonal flooding. Farming started in the predynastic period at the end of the [Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleolithic "Paleolithic"), after 10,000 BC. Staple food crops were grains such as wheat and barley, alongside industrial crops such as [flax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flax "Flax") and [papyrus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papyrus "Papyrus").[\[37\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Janick-37)[\[38\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-38) In [India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_India "Agriculture in India"), wheat, barley and [jujube](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jujube "Jujube") were domesticated by 9,000 BC, soon followed by sheep and goats.[\[39\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-gupta-39) Cattle, sheep and goats were domesticated in [Mehrgarh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mehrgarh "Mehrgarh") culture by 8,000–6,000 BC.[\[40\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Baber-40)[\[41\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-harrisandgosden385-41)[\[42\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Possehl-42) Cotton was cultivated by the 5th–4th millennium BC.[\[43\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-43) Archeological evidence indicates an animal-drawn [plough](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plough "Plough") from 2,500 BC in the [Indus Valley civilization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indus_Valley_civilisation "Indus Valley civilisation").[\[44\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-lal-44)

In China, from the 5th century BC, there was a nationwide [granary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Granary "Granary") system and widespread [silk farming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sericulture "Sericulture").[\[45\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-45) Water-powered grain mills were in use by the 1st century BC,[\[46\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-46) followed by irrigation.[\[47\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-47) By the late 2nd century, [heavy ploughs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heavy_plough "Heavy plough") had been developed with iron ploughshares and [mouldboards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mouldboard "Mouldboard").[\[48\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-greenberger_2006_11-12-48)[\[49\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-49) These spread westwards across Eurasia.[\[50\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-50) Asian rice was domesticated 8,200–13,500 years ago – depending on the [molecular clock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molecular_clock "Molecular clock") estimate that is used[\[51\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-pnas1-51)– on the Pearl River in southern China with a single genetic origin from the wild rice *[Oryza rufipogon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oryza_rufipogon "Oryza rufipogon")*.[\[52\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-nature1-52) In [Greece](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_ancient_Greece "Agriculture in ancient Greece") and [Rome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_ancient_Rome "Agriculture in ancient Rome"), the major cereals were wheat, emmer, and barley, alongside vegetables including peas, beans, and olives. Sheep and goats were kept mainly for dairy products.[\[53\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-koester_1995_p76-77-53)[\[54\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-White-54)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3d/Tomb_of_Nakht_%282%29.jpg/170px-Tomb_of_Nakht_%282%29.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Tomb_of_Nakht_\(2\).jpg)

Agricultural scenes of [threshing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Threshing "Threshing"), a grain store, harvesting with [sickles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sickle "Sickle"), digging, tree-cutting and ploughing from [ancient Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Egyptian_agriculture "Ancient Egyptian agriculture"). Tomb of [Nakht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nakht "Nakht"), 15th century BC

In the Americas, crops domesticated in [Mesoamerica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesoamerica "Mesoamerica") (apart from teosinte) include squash, beans, and [cacao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Theobroma_cacao "Theobroma cacao").[\[55\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Murphy2011-55) Cocoa was domesticated by the Mayo Chinchipe of the upper Amazon around 3,000 BC.[\[56\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-56) The [turkey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Domestic_turkey "Domestic turkey") was probably domesticated in Mexico or the American Southwest.[\[57\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Speller-57) The [Aztecs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aztec "Aztec") developed irrigation systems, formed [terraced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Terrace_\(agriculture\) "Terrace (agriculture)") hillsides, fertilized their soil, and developed [chinampas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinampa "Chinampa") or artificial islands. The [Mayas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maya_civilization "Maya civilization") used extensive canal and raised field systems to farm swampland from 400 BC.[\[58\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-58)[\[59\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-59)[\[60\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Spooner_2005_14694%E2%80%9399-60)[\[61\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-online-61)[\[62\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-John_Michael_Francis_2005-62) In South America agriculture may have begun about 9000 BC with the domestication of [squash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Squash_\(fruit\) "Squash (fruit)") (Cucurbita) and other plants.[\[63\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Piperno-63) [Coca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coca "Coca") was domesticated in the Andes, as were the peanut, tomato, tobacco, and [pineapple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pineapple "Pineapple").[\[55\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Murphy2011-55) Cotton was domesticated in Peru by 3,600 BC.[\[64\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Broudy1979_p81-64) Animals including [llamas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Llama "Llama"), [alpacas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alpaca "Alpaca"), and [guinea pigs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guinea_pig "Guinea pig") were domesticated there.[\[65\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-RischkowskyPilling2007-65) In [North America](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_agriculture_in_the_United_States "History of agriculture in the United States"), the indigenous people of the [East domesticated crops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Agricultural_Complex "Eastern Agricultural Complex") such as [sunflower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunflower "Sunflower"), tobacco,[\[66\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-66) squash and *[Chenopodium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chenopodium "Chenopodium")*.[\[67\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-67)[\[68\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-68) Wild foods including [wild rice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wild_rice "Wild rice") and [maple sugar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maple_sugar "Maple sugar") were harvested.[\[69\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Smith2013-69) The domesticated [strawberry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Strawberry "Strawberry") is a hybrid of a Chilean and a North American species, developed by breeding in Europe and North America.[\[70\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-70) The [indigenous people of the Southwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_the_prehistoric_Southwest "Agriculture in the prehistoric Southwest") and the [Pacific Northwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Northwest "Pacific Northwest") practiced [forest gardening](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_gardening "Forest gardening") and [fire-stick farming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire-stick_farming "Fire-stick farming"). The [natives controlled fire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_American_use_of_fire "Native American use of fire") on a regional scale to create a low-intensity [fire ecology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire_ecology "Fire ecology") that [sustained a low-density agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainable_agriculture "Sustainable agriculture") in loose rotation; a sort of "wild" [permaculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Permaculture "Permaculture").[\[71\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-71)[\[72\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-72)[\[73\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Cunningham2010-73)[\[74\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-74) A system of [companion planting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Companion_planting "Companion planting") called [the Three Sisters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three_Sisters_\(agriculture\) "Three Sisters (agriculture)") was developed in North America. The three crops were [winter squash](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winter_squash "Winter squash"), maize, and climbing beans.[\[75\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-wilson-75)[\[76\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-landon-76)

[Indigenous Australians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indigenous_Australians "Indigenous Australians"), long supposed to have been nomadic [hunter-gatherers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hunter-gatherers "Hunter-gatherers"), practiced systematic burning, possibly to enhance natural productivity in fire-stick farming.[\[77\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-77) Scholars have pointed out that hunter-gatherers need a productive environment to support gathering without cultivation. Because the forests of New Guinea have few food plants, early humans may have used "selective burning" to increase the productivity of the wild [karuka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Karuka "Karuka") fruit trees to support the hunter-gatherer way of life.[\[78\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-78)

The [Gunditjmara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gunditjmara "Gunditjmara") and other groups developed [eel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eel "Eel") farming and fish trapping systems from some 5,000 years ago.[\[79\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-79) There is evidence of 'intensification' across the whole continent over that period.[\[80\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-80) In two regions of Australia, the central west coast and eastern central, early farmers cultivated yams, native millet, and bush onions, possibly in permanent settlements.[\[33\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-b1-33)[\[81\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-81)

### Revolution

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=5 "Edit section: Revolution")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Crescenzi_calendar.jpg/220px-Crescenzi_calendar.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Crescenzi_calendar.jpg)

Agricultural calendar, c. 1470, from a manuscript of [Pietro de Crescenzi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pietro_de_Crescenzi "Pietro de Crescenzi")

In the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages "Middle Ages"), compared to the [Roman period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_ancient_Rome "Agriculture in ancient Rome"), agriculture in Western Europe became more focused on [self-sufficiency](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-sustainability "Self-sustainability"). The agricultural population under feudalism was typically organized into [manors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manorialism "Manorialism") consisting of several hundred or more acres of land presided over by a [lord of the manor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lord_of_the_manor "Lord of the manor") with a [Roman Catholic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic "Roman Catholic") church and priest.[\[82\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-NatGeographic2015-82)

Thanks to the exchange with the [Al-Andalus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Andalus "Al-Andalus") where the [Arab Agricultural Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Agricultural_Revolution "Arab Agricultural Revolution") was underway, European agriculture transformed, with improved techniques and the diffusion of crop plants, including the introduction of sugar, rice, cotton and fruit trees (such as the orange).[\[83\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Watson-83)

After 1492, the [Columbian exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Columbian_exchange "Columbian exchange") brought New World crops such as maize, potatoes, tomatoes, [sweet potatoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sweet_potato "Sweet potato"), and [manioc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manioc "Manioc") to Europe, and Old World crops such as wheat, barley, rice, and [turnips](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turnip "Turnip"), and livestock (including horses, cattle, sheep and goats) to the Americas.[\[84\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-84)

[Irrigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irrigation "Irrigation"), [crop rotation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crop_rotation "Crop rotation"), and [fertilizers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fertilizers "Fertilizers") advanced from the 17th century with the [British Agricultural Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_Agricultural_Revolution "British Agricultural Revolution"), allowing global population to rise significantly. Since 1900, agriculture in developed nations, and to a lesser extent in the developing world, has seen large rises in productivity as [mechanization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mechanized_farming "Mechanized farming") replaces human labor, and assisted by [synthetic fertilizers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Synthetic_fertilizer "Synthetic fertilizer"), pesticides, and [selective breeding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Selective_breeding "Selective breeding"). The [Haber-Bosch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haber-Bosch "Haber-Bosch") method allowed the synthesis of [ammonium nitrate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ammonium_nitrate "Ammonium nitrate") fertilizer on an industrial scale, greatly increasing [crop yields](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crop_yields "Crop yields") and sustaining a further increase in global population.[\[85\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-85)[\[86\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-86)

Modern agriculture has raised or encountered ecological, political, and economic issues including [water pollution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_pollution "Water pollution"), [biofuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biofuel "Biofuel"), [genetically modified organisms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetically_modified_organism "Genetically modified organism"), [tariffs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tariff "Tariff") and [farm subsidies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_subsidy "Agricultural subsidy"), leading to alternative approaches such as the [organic movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organic_movement "Organic movement").[\[87\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-motherjones1-87)[\[88\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-smh.com.au-88) Unsustainable farming practices in North America led to the [Dust Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dust_Bowl "Dust Bowl") of the 1930s.[\[89\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-89)

## Types

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=6 "Edit section: Types")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/40/Reindeer_herding.jpg/220px-Reindeer_herding.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Reindeer_herding.jpg)

[Reindeer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reindeer "Reindeer") herds form the basis of pastoral agriculture for several Arctic and Subarctic peoples.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/76/Unload_wheat_by_the_combine_Claas_Lexion_584.jpg/220px-Unload_wheat_by_the_combine_Claas_Lexion_584.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Unload_wheat_by_the_combine_Claas_Lexion_584.jpg)

[Harvesting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvest "Harvest") wheat with a [combine harvester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combine_harvester "Combine harvester") accompanied by a tractor and trailer

[Pastoralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pastoralism "Pastoralism") involves managing domesticated animals. In [nomadic pastoralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomadic_pastoralism "Nomadic pastoralism"), herds of livestock are moved from place to place in search of pasture, fodder, and water. This type of farming is practiced in arid and semi-arid regions of [Sahara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sahara "Sahara"), Central Asia and some parts of India.[\[90\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-90)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b4/Manuring_a_vegetable_garden.jpg/220px-Manuring_a_vegetable_garden.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Manuring_a_vegetable_garden.jpg)

Spreading manure by hand in Zambia

In [shifting cultivation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shifting_cultivation "Shifting cultivation"), a small area of forest is cleared by cutting and burning the trees. The cleared land is used for growing crops for a few years until the soil becomes too infertile, and the area is abandoned. Another patch of land is selected and the process is repeated. This type of farming is practiced mainly in areas with abundant rainfall where the forest regenerates quickly. This practice is used in Northeast India, Southeast Asia, and the Amazon Basin.[\[91\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-91)

[Subsistence farming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsistence_farming "Subsistence farming") is practiced to satisfy family or local needs alone, with little left over for transport elsewhere. It is intensively practiced in Monsoon Asia and South-East Asia.[\[92\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-92) An estimated 2.5 billion subsistence farmers worked in 2018, cultivating about 60% of the earth's [arable land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arable_land "Arable land").[\[93\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-93)

[Intensive farming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intensive_farming "Intensive farming") is cultivation to maximize productivity, with a low fallow ratio and a high use of inputs (water, fertilizer, pesticide and automation). It is practiced mainly in developed countries.[\[94\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-94)[\[95\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-bbcFactSheet-95)

## Contemporary agriculture

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=7 "Edit section: Contemporary agriculture")]

### Status

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=8 "Edit section: Status")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/de/Inherent_Land_Quality_Map.png/354px-Inherent_Land_Quality_Map.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Inherent_Land_Quality_Map.png)

Suitability for agriculture of land around the world (US Department of Agriculture, 1998)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/37/Employment_In_Agriculture%2C_Forestry_And_Fishing_By_Region.svg/221px-Employment_In_Agriculture%2C_Forestry_And_Fishing_By_Region.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Employment_In_Agriculture,_Forestry_And_Fishing_By_Region.svg)

Recent trends of employment in agriculture (including forestry and fishing) by region

From the twentieth century onwards, intensive agriculture increased crop productivity. It substituted synthetic fertilizers and pesticides for labor, but caused increased water pollution, and often involved farm subsidies. [Soil degradation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soil_retrogression_and_degradation "Soil retrogression and degradation") and diseases such as [stem rust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stem_rust "Stem rust") are major concerns globally;[\[96\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-96) approximately 40% of the world's agricultural land is seriously degraded.[\[97\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-97)[\[98\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-98) In recent years there has been a backlash against the [environmental effects](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_awareness "Environmental awareness") of conventional agriculture, resulting in the [organic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organic_farming "Organic farming"), [regenerative](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regenerative_agriculture "Regenerative agriculture"), and [sustainable agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainable_agriculture "Sustainable agriculture") movements.[\[87\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-motherjones1-87)[\[99\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-99) One of the major forces behind this movement has been the [European Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Union "European Union"), which first certified [organic food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organic_food "Organic food") in 1991 and began reform of its [Common Agricultural Policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Agricultural_Policy "Common Agricultural Policy") (CAP) in 2005 to phase out commodity-linked farm subsidies,[\[100\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-100) also known as [decoupling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Decoupling_and_re-coupling "Decoupling and re-coupling"). The growth of organic farming has renewed research in alternative technologies such as [integrated pest management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Integrated_pest_management "Integrated pest management"), selective breeding,[\[101\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-101) and [controlled-environment agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Controlled-environment_agriculture "Controlled-environment agriculture").[\[102\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:1-102)[\[103\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-103) There are concerns about the lower yield associated with [organic farming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organic_farming "Organic farming") and its impact on global [food security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_security "Food security").[\[104\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-104) Recent mainstream technological developments include [genetically modified food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetically_modified_food "Genetically modified food").[\[105\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-105)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8a/Agricultural_output_of_China.svg/220px-Agricultural_output_of_China.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Agricultural_output_of_China.svg)

Development of agricultural output of China in 2015 US$ since 1961

By 2015, the agricultural output of China was the largest in the world, followed by the European Union, India and the United States.[\[106\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-UNCTAD2017-106) Economists measure the [total factor productivity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Total_factor_productivity "Total factor productivity") of agriculture, according to which agriculture in the United States is roughly 1.7 times more productive than it was in 1948.[\[107\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-107)

Agriculture employed 873 million people in 2021, or 27% of the global workforce, compared with 1 027 million (or 40%) in 2000. The share of agriculture in global GDP was stable at around 4% since 2000–2023.[\[108\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:14-108)

Despite increases in agricultural production and productivity,[\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:3-109) between 702 and 828 million people were affected by hunger in 2021.[\[110\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-110) Food insecurity and malnutrition can be the result of conflict, climate extremes and variability and economic swings.[\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:3-109) It can also be caused by a country's structural characteristics such as income status and natural resource endowments as well as its political economy.[\[109\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:3-109)

Pesticide use in agriculture went up 62% between 2000 and 2021, with the Americas accounting for half the use in 2021.[\[108\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:14-108)

The [International Fund for Agricultural Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Fund_for_Agricultural_Development "International Fund for Agricultural Development") posits that an increase in [smallholder agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smallholding "Smallholding") may be part of the solution to concerns about [food prices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_prices "Food prices") and overall [food security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_security "Food security"), given the favorable experience of Vietnam.[\[111\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-111)

### Workforce

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=9 "Edit section: Workforce")]

See also: [Gender roles in agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gender_roles_in_agriculture "Gender roles in agriculture")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/91/Employment_In_Agriculture%2C_Forestry_And_Fishing_%282021%29.svg/220px-Employment_In_Agriculture%2C_Forestry_And_Fishing_%282021%29.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Employment_In_Agriculture,_Forestry_And_Fishing_\(2021\).svg)

Worldwide employment In agriculture, forestry and fishing in 2021

Agriculture provides about one-quarter of all global employment, more than half in sub-Saharan Africa and almost 60 percent in low-income countries.[\[112\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-112) As countries develop, other jobs have historically pulled workers away from agriculture, and labor-saving innovations increase agricultural productivity by reducing labor requirements per unit of output.[\[113\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-113)[\[114\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-114)[\[115\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-115) Over time, a combination of labor supply and labor demand trends have driven down the share of population employed in agriculture.[\[116\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-116)[\[117\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-117)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/80/Transition_from_agriculture_to_developed_economy.svg/220px-Transition_from_agriculture_to_developed_economy.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Transition_from_agriculture_to_developed_economy.svg)

On the [three-sector theory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Three-sector_theory "Three-sector theory"), the proportion of people working in agriculture (left-hard bar in each group, green) falls as an economy becomes more developed.

During the 16th century in Europe, between 55 and 75% of the population was engaged in agriculture; by the 19th century, this had dropped to between 35 and 65%.[\[118\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-118) In the same countries today, the figure is less than 10%.[\[119\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-LaborForce-119) At the start of the 21st century, some one billion people, or over 1/3 of the available work force, were employed in agriculture. This constitutes approximately 70% of the global employment of children, and in many countries constitutes the largest percentage of women of any industry.[\[120\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ILO-120) The service sector overtook the agricultural sector as the largest global employer in 2007.[\[121\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-121)

In many developed countries, immigrants help fill labor shortages in high-value agriculture activities that are difficult to mechanize.[\[122\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-122) Foreign farm workers from mostly Eastern Europe, North Africa and South Asia constituted around one-third of the salaried agricultural workforce in Spain, Italy, Greece and Portugal in 2013.[\[123\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-123)[\[124\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-124)[\[125\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-125)[\[126\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-126) In the United States of America, more than half of all hired farmworkers (roughly 450,000 workers) were immigrants in 2019, although the number of new immigrants arriving in the country to work in agriculture has fallen by 75 percent in recent years and rising wages indicate this has led to a major labor shortage on U.S. farms.[\[127\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-127)[\[128\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-128)

#### Women in agriculture

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=10 "Edit section: Women in agriculture")]

Around the world, women make up a large share of the population employed in agriculture.[\[129\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:4-129) This share is growing in all developing regions except East and Southeast Asia where women already make up about 50 percent of the agricultural workforce.[\[129\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:4-129) Women make up 47 percent of the agricultural workforce in sub-Saharan Africa, a rate that has not changed significantly in the past few decades.[\[129\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:4-129) However, the [Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_and_Agriculture_Organization "Food and Agriculture Organization") (FAO) posits that the roles and responsibilities of women in agriculture may be changing – for example, from subsistence farming to wage employment, and from contributing household members to primary producers in the context of male-out-migration.[\[129\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:4-129)

In general, women account for a greater share of agricultural employment at lower levels of economic development, as inadequate education, limited access to basic infrastructure and markets, high unpaid work burden and poor rural employment opportunities outside agriculture severely limit women's opportunities for off-farm work.[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:13-130)

Women who work in agricultural production tend to do so under highly unfavorable conditions. They tend to be concentrated in the poorest countries, where alternative livelihoods are not available, and they maintain the intensity of their work in conditions of climate-induced weather shocks and in situations of conflict. Women are less likely to participate as entrepreneurs and independent farmers and are engaged in the production of less lucrative crops.[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:13-130)

The gender gap in land productivity between female- and male managed farms of the same size is 24 percent. On average, women earn 18.4 percent less than men in wage employment in agriculture; this means that women receive 82 cents for every dollar earned by men. Progress has been slow in closing gaps in women's access to irrigation and in ownership of livestock, too.[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:13-130)

Women in agriculture still have significantly less access than men to inputs, including improved seeds, fertilizers and mechanized equipment. On a positive note, the gender gap in access to mobile internet in low- and middle-income countries fell from 25 percent to 16 percent between 2017 and 2021, and the gender gap in access to bank accounts narrowed from 9 to 6 percentage points. Women are as likely as men to adopt new technologies when the necessary enabling factors are put in place and they have equal access to complementary resources.[\[130\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:13-130)

### Safety

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=11 "Edit section: Safety")]

Main article: [Agricultural safety and health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_safety_and_health "Agricultural safety and health")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/10/Ford_Tractor_with_ROPS_bar_fitted.JPG/220px-Ford_Tractor_with_ROPS_bar_fitted.JPG)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Ford_Tractor_with_ROPS_bar_fitted.JPG)

[Rollover protection bar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rollover_protection_system "Rollover protection system") [retrofitted](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Retrofit "Retrofit") to a mid-20th century [Fordson tractor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fordson_tractor "Fordson tractor")

Agriculture, specifically farming, remains a hazardous industry, and farmers worldwide remain at high risk of work-related injuries, lung disease, [noise-induced hearing loss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Noise-induced_hearing_loss "Noise-induced hearing loss"), skin diseases, as well as certain cancers related to chemical use and prolonged sun exposure. On [industrialized farms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Industrial_agriculture "Industrial agriculture"), injuries frequently involve the use of [agricultural machinery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_machinery "Agricultural machinery"), and a common cause of fatal agricultural injuries in developed countries is [tractor rollovers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rollover_protection_structure "Rollover protection structure").[\[131\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-aginjury-131) Pesticides and other chemicals used in farming can be [hazardous to worker health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_effects_of_pesticides "Health effects of pesticides"), and workers exposed to pesticides may experience illness or have children with birth defects.[\[132\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-NIOSH_pest-132) As an industry in which families commonly share in work and live on the farm itself, entire families can be at risk for injuries, illness, and death.[\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-NIOSH_Agri-133) Ages 0–6 may be an especially vulnerable population in agriculture;[\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-WeicheltGorucu2018-134) common causes of fatal injuries among young farm workers include drowning, machinery and motor accidents, including with all-terrain vehicles.[\[133\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-NIOSH_Agri-133)[\[134\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-WeicheltGorucu2018-134)[\[135\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-135)

The [International Labor Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Labour_Organization "International Labour Organization") considers agriculture "one of the most hazardous of all economic sectors".[\[120\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ILO-120) It estimates that the annual work-related death toll among agricultural employees is at least 170,000, twice the average rate of other jobs. In addition, incidences of death, injury and illness related to agricultural activities often go unreported.[\[136\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ILO2-136) The organization has developed the [Safety and Health in Agriculture Convention, 2001](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safety_and_Health_in_Agriculture_Convention,_2001 "Safety and Health in Agriculture Convention, 2001"), which covers the range of risks in the agriculture occupation, the prevention of these risks and the role that individuals and organizations engaged in agriculture should play.[\[120\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ILO-120)

In the United States, agriculture has been identified by the [National Institute for Occupational Safety and Health](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Institute_for_Occupational_Safety_and_Health "National Institute for Occupational Safety and Health") as a priority industry sector in the [National Occupational Research Agenda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Occupational_Research_Agenda "National Occupational Research Agenda") to identify and provide intervention strategies for occupational health and safety issues.[\[137\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-137)[\[138\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-138) In the European Union, the [European Agency for Safety and Health at Work](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Agency_for_Safety_and_Health_at_Work "European Agency for Safety and Health at Work") has issued guidelines on implementing health and safety directives in agriculture, livestock farming, horticulture, and forestry.[\[139\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-139) The Agricultural Safety and Health Council of America (ASHCA) also holds a yearly summit to discuss safety.[\[140\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-140)

### Crop cultivation systems

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=13 "Edit section: Crop cultivation systems")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3c/An_example_of_slash_and_burn_agriculture_practice_Thailand.jpg/220px-An_example_of_slash_and_burn_agriculture_practice_Thailand.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:An_example_of_slash_and_burn_agriculture_practice_Thailand.jpg)

[Slash and burn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slash_and_burn "Slash and burn") shifting cultivation, Thailand

Cropping systems vary among farms depending on the available resources and constraints; geography and climate of the farm; government policy; economic, social and political pressures; and the philosophy and culture of the farmer.[\[142\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FAO_FS-142)[\[143\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-PCP_APS-143)

Shifting cultivation (or [slash and burn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slash_and_burn "Slash and burn")) is a system in which forests are burnt, releasing nutrients to support cultivation of annual and then [perennial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Perennial_plant "Perennial plant") crops for a period of several years.[\[144\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CS-144) Then the plot is left [fallow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fallow "Fallow") to regrow forest, and the farmer moves to a new plot, returning after many more years (10–20). This fallow period is shortened if population density grows, requiring the input of nutrients (fertilizer or [manure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manure "Manure")) and some manual [pest control](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pest_control "Pest control"). Annual cultivation is the next phase of intensity in which there is no fallow period. This requires even greater nutrient and pest control inputs.[\[144\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CS-144)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/86/Intercropping_coconut_n_Tagetes_erecta.jpg/220px-Intercropping_coconut_n_Tagetes_erecta.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Intercropping_coconut_n_Tagetes_erecta.jpg)

[Intercropping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercropping "Intercropping") of [coconut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coconut "Coconut") and [Mexican marigold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagetes_erecta "Tagetes erecta")

Further industrialization led to the use of [monocultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monoculture "Monoculture"), when one [cultivar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultivar "Cultivar") is planted on a large acreage. Because of the low [biodiversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity "Biodiversity"), nutrient use is uniform and pests tend to build up, necessitating the greater use of [pesticides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pesticide "Pesticide") and fertilizers.[\[143\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-PCP_APS-143) [Multiple cropping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiple_cropping "Multiple cropping"), in which several crops are grown sequentially in one year, and [intercropping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercropping "Intercropping"), when several crops are grown at the same time, are other kinds of annual cropping systems known as [polycultures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polyculture "Polyculture").[\[144\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CS-144)

In [subtropical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subtropics "Subtropics") and [arid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arid "Arid") environments, the timing and extent of agriculture may be limited by rainfall, either not allowing multiple [annual crops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annual_plant "Annual plant") in a year, or requiring irrigation. In all of these environments perennial crops are grown (coffee, chocolate) and systems are practiced such as [agroforestry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agroforestry "Agroforestry"). In [temperate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperateness "Temperateness") environments, where ecosystems were predominantly [grassland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grassland "Grassland") or [prairie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prairie "Prairie"), highly productive annual farming is the dominant agricultural system.[\[144\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CS-144)

Important categories of food crops include cereals, legumes, forage, fruits and vegetables.[\[145\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FAO-145) [Natural fibers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Natural_fiber "Natural fiber") include cotton, [wool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wool "Wool"), [hemp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hemp "Hemp"), silk and [flax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flax "Flax").[\[146\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-146) Specific crops are cultivated in distinct [growing regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Growing_region "Growing region") throughout the world. Production is listed in millions of metric tons, based on [FAO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_and_Agriculture_Organization "Food and Agriculture Organization") estimates.[\[145\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FAO-145)
