### Livestock production systems

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=14 "Edit section: Livestock production systems")]

Main articles: [Livestock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Livestock "Livestock") and [Animal husbandry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_husbandry "Animal husbandry")

See also: [List of domesticated animals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_domesticated_animals "List of domesticated animals")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/72/Hog_confinement_barn_interior.jpg/220px-Hog_confinement_barn_interior.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Hog_confinement_barn_interior.jpg)

[Intensively farmed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intensive_animal_farming "Intensive animal farming") pigs

Animal husbandry is the breeding and raising of animals for meat, milk, [eggs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egg_\(food\) "Egg (food)"), or [wool](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wool "Wool"), and for work and transport.[\[147\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Clutton-Brock-147) [Working animals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Working_animal "Working animal"), including horses, [mules](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mule "Mule"), [oxen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ox "Ox"), [water buffalo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_buffalo "Water buffalo"), camels, llamas, alpacas, donkeys, and dogs, have for centuries been used to help cultivate fields, [harvest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harvest "Harvest") crops, wrangle other animals, and transport farm products to buyers.[\[148\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-148)

Livestock production systems can be defined based on feed source, as grassland-based, mixed, and landless.[\[149\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FAO_lps-149) As of 2010[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit), 30% of Earth's ice- and water-free area was used for producing livestock, with the sector employing approximately 1.3 billion people. Between the 1960s and the 2000s, there was a significant increase in livestock production, both by numbers and by carcass weight, especially among beef, pigs and chickens, the latter of which had production increased by almost a factor of 10. Non-meat animals, such as milk cows and egg-producing chickens, also showed significant production increases. Global cattle, sheep and goat populations are expected to continue to increase sharply through 2050.[\[150\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-LP-150) [Aquaculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquaculture "Aquaculture") or fish farming, the production of fish for human consumption in confined operations, is one of the fastest growing sectors of food production, growing at an average of 9% a year between 1975 and 2007.[\[151\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-151)

During the second half of the 20th century, producers using selective breeding focused on creating livestock [breeds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breed "Breed") and [crossbreeds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crossbreed "Crossbreed") that increased production, while mostly disregarding the need to preserve [genetic diversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_diversity "Genetic diversity"). This trend has led to a significant decrease in genetic diversity and resources among livestock breeds, leading to a corresponding decrease in disease resistance and local adaptations previously found among traditional breeds.[\[152\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-152)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a2/Broiler_Chicks.jpg/220px-Broiler_Chicks.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Broiler_Chicks.jpg)

Raising chickens intensively for meat in a broiler house

Grassland based livestock production relies upon plant material such as [shrubland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shrubland "Shrubland"), [rangeland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rangeland "Rangeland"), and [pastures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Managed_intensive_rotational_grazing "Managed intensive rotational grazing") for feeding [ruminant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ruminant "Ruminant") animals. Outside nutrient inputs may be used, however manure is returned directly to the grassland as a major nutrient source. This system is particularly important in areas where crop production is not feasible because of climate or soil, representing 30–40 million pastoralists.[\[144\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CS-144) Mixed production systems use grassland, [fodder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fodder "Fodder") crops and grain feed crops as feed for ruminant and monogastric (one stomach; mainly chickens and pigs) livestock. Manure is typically recycled in mixed systems as a fertilizer for crops.[\[149\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FAO_lps-149)

Landless systems rely upon feed from outside the farm, representing the de-linking of crop and livestock production found more prevalently in [Organization for Economic Co-operation and Development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organisation_for_Economic_Co-operation_and_Development "Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development") member countries. Synthetic fertilizers are more heavily relied upon for crop production and manure use becomes a challenge as well as a source for pollution.[\[149\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FAO_lps-149) Industrialized countries use these operations to produce much of the global supplies of poultry and pork. Scientists estimate that 75% of the growth in livestock production between 2003 and 2030 will be in [confined animal feeding operations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Confined_animal_feeding_operations "Confined animal feeding operations"), sometimes called [factory farming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Factory_farming "Factory farming"). Much of this growth is happening in developing countries in Asia, with much smaller amounts of growth in Africa.[\[150\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-LP-150) Some of the practices used in commercial livestock production, including the usage of [growth hormones](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Growth_hormone "Growth hormone"), are controversial.[\[153\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-153)

### Production practices

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=15 "Edit section: Production practices")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/0/04/Fendt_Tractor_Ripping_up_Kulin.jpg/220px-Fendt_Tractor_Ripping_up_Kulin.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Fendt_Tractor_Ripping_up_Kulin.jpg)

[Tilling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tillage "Tillage") an arable field

Further information: [Tillage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tillage "Tillage"), [Crop rotation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crop_rotation "Crop rotation"), and [Irrigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irrigation "Irrigation")

Tillage is the practice of breaking up the soil with tools such as the plow or [harrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harrow_\(tool\) "Harrow (tool)") to prepare for planting, for nutrient incorporation, or for pest control. Tillage varies in intensity from conventional to [no-till](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No-till_farming "No-till farming"). It can improve productivity by warming the soil, incorporating fertilizer and controlling weeds, but also renders soil more prone to erosion, triggers the decomposition of organic matter releasing CO2, and reduces the abundance and diversity of soil organisms.[\[154\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Soil_nutrient-154)[\[155\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-PCP_Tillage-155)

Pest control includes the management of weeds, insects, [mites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mite "Mite"), and diseases. Chemical (pesticides), biological ([biocontrol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biocontrol "Biocontrol")), mechanical (tillage), and cultural practices are used. Cultural practices include crop rotation, [culling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culling "Culling"), [cover crops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cover_crop "Cover crop"), intercropping, [composting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compost "Compost"), avoidance, and resistance. Integrated pest management attempts to use all of these methods to keep pest populations below the number which would cause economic loss, and recommends pesticides as a last resort.[\[156\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-PCP_Pest-156)

[Nutrient management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nutrient_management "Nutrient management") includes both the source of nutrient inputs for crop and livestock production, and the method of use of manure produced by livestock. Nutrient inputs can be chemical inorganic fertilizers, manure, [green manure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_manure "Green manure"), compost and minerals.[\[157\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-PCP_Soil-157) Crop nutrient use may also be managed using cultural techniques such as crop rotation or a [fallow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fallow "Fallow") period. Manure is used either by holding livestock where the feed crop is growing, such as in managed intensive rotational grazing, or [by spreading](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manure_spreader "Manure spreader") either dry or liquid formulations of manure on cropland or [pastures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pasture "Pasture").[\[154\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Soil_nutrient-154)[\[158\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CS_nutrient-158)

[Water management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_management "Water management") is needed where rainfall is insufficient or variable, which occurs to some degree in most regions of the world.[\[144\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CS-144) Some farmers use irrigation to supplement rainfall. In other areas such as the [Great Plains](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Plains "Great Plains") in the U.S. and Canada, farmers use a fallow year to conserve soil moisture for the following year.[\[159\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-PCP_Water-159) Recent technological innovations in precision agriculture allow for water status monitoring and automate water usage, leading to more efficient management.[\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:5-160) Agriculture represents 70% of freshwater use worldwide.[\[161\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Pimentel_water-161) However, water withdrawal ratios for agriculture vary significantly by income level. In least developed countries and landlocked developing countries, water withdrawal ratios for agriculture are as high as 90 percent of total water withdrawals and about 60 percent in [Small Island Developing States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_Island_Developing_States "Small Island Developing States").[\[162\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:9-162)

According to 2014 report by the [International Food Policy Research Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Food_Policy_Research_Institute "International Food Policy Research Institute"), agricultural technologies will have the greatest impact on food production if adopted in combination with each other. Using a model that assessed how eleven technologies could impact agricultural productivity, food security and trade by 2050, the International Food Policy Research Institute found that the number of people at risk from hunger could be reduced by as much as 40% and food prices could be reduced by almost half.[\[163\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ifpri-163)

[Payment for ecosystem services](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Payment_for_ecosystem_services "Payment for ecosystem services") is a method of providing additional incentives to encourage farmers to conserve some aspects of the environment. Measures might include paying for reforestation upstream of a city, to improve the supply of fresh water.[\[164\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-164)

### Agricultural automation

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=16 "Edit section: Agricultural automation")]

Different definitions exist for agricultural automation and for the variety of tools and technologies that are used to automate production. One view is that agricultural automation refers to autonomous navigation by robots without human intervention.[\[165\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-165) Alternatively, it is defined as the accomplishment of production tasks through mobile, autonomous, decision-making, mechatronic devices.[\[166\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-166) However, [FAO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_and_Agriculture_Organization "Food and Agriculture Organization") finds that these definitions do not capture all the aspects and forms of automation, such as robotic milking machines that are static, most motorized machinery that automates the performing of agricultural operations, and digital tools (e.g., sensors) that automate only diagnosis.[\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:5-160) FAO defines agricultural automation as the use of machinery and equipment in agricultural operations to improve their diagnosis, decision-making or performing, reducing the drudgery of agricultural work or improving the timeliness, and potentially the precision, of agricultural operations.[\[167\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:6-167)

The technological evolution in agriculture has involved a progressive move from manual tools to animal traction, to motorized mechanization, to digital equipment and finally, to robotics with artificial intelligence (AI).[\[167\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:6-167) Motorized mechanization using engine power automates the performance of agricultural operations such as ploughing and milking.[\[168\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:7-168) With digital automation technologies, it also becomes possible to automate diagnosis and decision-making of agricultural operations.[\[167\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:6-167) For example, autonomous crop robots can harvest and seed crops, while drones can gather information to help automate input application.[\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:5-160) Precision agriculture often employs such automation technologies.[\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:5-160) Motorized machines are increasingly complemented, or even superseded, by new digital equipment that automates diagnosis and decision-making.[\[168\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:7-168) A conventional tractor, for example, can be converted into an automated vehicle allowing it to sow a field autonomously.[\[168\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:7-168)

Motorized mechanization has increased significantly across the world in recent years, although reliable global data with broad country coverage exist only for tractors and only up to 2009.[\[169\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-169) Sub-Saharan Africa is the only region where the adoption of motorized mechanization has stalled over the past decades.[\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:5-160)[\[170\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-170)

Automation technologies are increasingly used for managing livestock, though evidence on adoption is lacking. Global automatic milking system sales have increased over recent years, but adoption is likely mostly in Northern Europe,[\[171\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-171) and likely almost absent in low- and middle-income countries. Automated feeding machines for both cows and poultry also exist, but data and evidence regarding their adoption trends and drivers is likewise scarce.[\[172\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-172)[\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:5-160)

Measuring the overall employment impacts of agricultural automation is difficult because it requires large amounts of data tracking all the transformations and the associated reallocation of workers both upstream and downstream.[\[167\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:6-167) While automation technologies reduce labor needs for the newly automated tasks, they also generate new labor demand for other tasks, such as equipment maintenance and operation.[\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:5-160) Agricultural automation can also stimulate employment by allowing producers to expand production and by creating other agrifood systems jobs.[\[173\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:8-173) This is especially true when it happens in context of rising scarcity of rural labor, as is the case in high-income countries and many middle-income countries.[\[173\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:8-173) On the other hand, if forcedly promoted, for example through government subsidies in contexts of abundant rural labor, it can lead to labor displacement and falling or stagnant wages, particularly affecting poor and low-skilled workers.[\[173\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:8-173)

### Effects of climate change on yields

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=17 "Edit section: Effects of climate change on yields")]

Main article: [Effects of climate change on agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_climate_change_on_agriculture "Effects of climate change on agriculture")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/8/8d/Soil_moisture_and_climate_change.svg/300px-Soil_moisture_and_climate_change.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Soil_moisture_and_climate_change.svg)

The sixth IPCC Assessment Report projects changes in average soil moisture at 2.0 °C of warming, as measured in [standard deviations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_deviation "Standard deviation") from the 1850 to 1900 baseline.

[Climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change "Climate change") and agriculture are interrelated on a global scale. [Climate change affects agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_climate_change_on_agriculture "Effects of climate change on agriculture") through changes in [average temperatures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Instrumental_temperature_record "Instrumental temperature record"), rainfall, and [weather extremes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Extreme_weather "Extreme weather") (like storms and heat waves); changes in pests and diseases; changes in atmospheric [carbon dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_dioxide "Carbon dioxide") and ground-level [ozone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ozone "Ozone") concentrations; changes in the nutritional quality of some foods;[\[174\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-science-news-174) and changes in [sea level](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Current_sea_level_rise "Current sea level rise").[\[175\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-175) Global warming is already affecting agriculture, with effects unevenly distributed across the world.[\[176\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-porter_summary-176)

In a 2022 report, the [Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intergovernmental_Panel_on_Climate_Change "Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change") describes how human-induced warming has slowed growth of agricultural productivity over the past 50 years in mid and low latitudes.[\[177\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:10-177) Methane emissions have negatively impacted crop yields by increasing temperatures and surface ozone concentrations.[\[177\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:10-177) Warming is also negatively affecting crop and grassland quality and harvest stability.[\[177\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:10-177) [Ocean warming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ocean_warming "Ocean warming") has decreased sustainable yields of some wild fish populations while ocean acidification and warming have already affected farmed aquatic species.[\[177\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:10-177) Climate change will probably increase the risk of [food insecurity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_insecurity "Food insecurity") for some vulnerable groups, such as the [poor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poverty "Poverty").[\[178\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-178)

## Crop alteration and biotechnology

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=18 "Edit section: Crop alteration and biotechnology")]

### Plant breeding

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=19 "Edit section: Plant breeding")]

Main article: [Plant breeding](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plant_breeding "Plant breeding")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/2/29/Wheat_selection_k10183-1.jpg/220px-Wheat_selection_k10183-1.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Wheat_selection_k10183-1.jpg)

Wheat cultivar tolerant of high [salinity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salinity "Salinity") (left) compared with non-tolerant variety

Crop alteration has been practiced by humankind for thousands of years, since the beginning of civilization. Altering crops through breeding practices changes the genetic make-up of a plant to develop crops with more beneficial characteristics for humans, for example, larger fruits or seeds, drought-tolerance, or resistance to pests. Significant advances in plant breeding ensued after the work of geneticist [Gregor Mendel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gregor_Mendel "Gregor Mendel"). His work on [dominant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dominant_allele "Dominant allele") and [recessive alleles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recessive_allele "Recessive allele"), although initially largely ignored for almost 50 years, gave plant breeders a better understanding of genetics and breeding techniques. Crop breeding includes techniques such as plant selection with desirable traits, [self-pollination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-pollination "Self-pollination") and [cross-pollination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cross-pollination "Cross-pollination"), and molecular techniques that genetically modify the organism.[\[179\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-179)

Domestication of plants has, over the centuries increased yield, improved disease resistance and [drought tolerance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Drought_tolerance "Drought tolerance"), eased harvest and improved the taste and nutritional value of crop plants. Careful selection and breeding have had enormous effects on the characteristics of crop plants. Plant selection and breeding in the 1920s and 1930s improved pasture (grasses and clover) in New Zealand. Extensive X-ray and ultraviolet induced mutagenesis efforts (i.e. primitive genetic engineering) during the 1950s produced the modern commercial varieties of grains such as wheat, corn (maize) and barley.[\[180\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-180)[\[181\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-181)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Seedlings_in_Green_House.jpg/220px-Seedlings_in_Green_House.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Seedlings_in_Green_House.jpg)

Seedlings in a green house. This is what it looks like when seedlings are growing from plant breeding.

The [Green Revolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Revolution "Green Revolution") popularized the use of conventional [hybridization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hybrid_\(biology\) "Hybrid (biology)") to sharply increase yield by creating "high-yielding varieties". For example, average yields of corn (maize) in the US have increased from around 2.5 tons per hectare (t/ha) (40 bushels per acre) in 1900 to about 9.4 t/ha (150 bushels per acre) in 2001. Similarly, worldwide average wheat yields have increased from less than 1 t/ha in 1900 to more than 2.5 t/ha in 1990. South American average wheat yields are around 2 t/ha, African under 1 t/ha, and Egypt and Arabia up to 3.5 to 4 t/ha with irrigation. In contrast, the average wheat yield in countries such as France is over 8 t/ha. Variations in yields are due mainly to variation in climate, genetics, and the level of intensive farming techniques (use of fertilizers, chemical pest control, and growth control to avoid lodging).[\[182\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-182)[\[183\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-183)[\[184\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-184)

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/b/b8/IP_Related_Innovation_in_Agriculture_2000-2021.png/220px-IP_Related_Innovation_in_Agriculture_2000-2021.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:IP_Related_Innovation_in_Agriculture_2000-2021.png)

Increase of [intellectual property](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intellectual_property "Intellectual property") protection for agri inventions, as seen in the total number of [patents](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patent "Patent"), [utility models](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utility_model "Utility model") and [plant varieties](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Variety_\(botany\) "Variety (botany)") equivalent protection systems applied for on agricultural innovation worldwide.

Investments into [innovation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Innovation "Innovation") for agriculture are long term. This is because it takes time for research to become commercialized and for technology to be adapted to meet multiple regions’ needs, as well as meet national guidelines before being adopted and planted in a farmer's fields. For instance, it took at least 60 years from the introduction of [hybrid corn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heterosis "Heterosis") technology before its adoption became widespread.[\[185\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:15-185)[\[186\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-186)

Agricultural innovation developed for the specific agroecological conditions of one region is not easily transferred and used in another region with different agroecological conditions. Instead, the innovation would have to be adapted to the specific conditions of that other region and respect its [biodiversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity "Biodiversity") and environmental requirements and guidelines. Some such adaptations can be seen through the steadily increasing number of plant varieties protected under the plant variety protection instrument administered by the [International Union for the Protection of New Varieties of Plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Union_for_the_Protection_of_New_Varieties_of_Plants "International Union for the Protection of New Varieties of Plants") (UPOV).[\[185\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:15-185)

### Genetic engineering

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=20 "Edit section: Genetic engineering")]

Main article: [Genetic engineering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetic_engineering "Genetic engineering")

See also: [Genetically modified food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetically_modified_food "Genetically modified food"), [Genetically modified crops](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetically_modified_crops "Genetically modified crops"), [Regulation of the release of genetic modified organisms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regulation_of_the_release_of_genetic_modified_organisms "Regulation of the release of genetic modified organisms"), and [Genetically modified food controversies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetically_modified_food_controversies "Genetically modified food controversies")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e3/CSIRO_ScienceImage_382_Genetically_Modified_Potatoes.jpg/220px-CSIRO_ScienceImage_382_Genetically_Modified_Potatoes.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:CSIRO_ScienceImage_382_Genetically_Modified_Potatoes.jpg)

[Genetically modified](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetically_modified_crops "Genetically modified crops") potato plants (left) resist virus diseases that damage unmodified plants (right).

Genetically modified organisms (GMO) are [organisms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organism "Organism") whose [genetic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genetics "Genetics") material has been altered by genetic engineering techniques generally known as [recombinant DNA technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recombinant_DNA_technology "Recombinant DNA technology"). Genetic engineering has expanded the genes available to breeders to use in creating desired germlines for new crops. Increased durability, nutritional content, insect and virus resistance and herbicide tolerance are a few of the attributes bred into crops through genetic engineering.[\[187\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-187) For some, GMO crops cause [food safety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_safety "Food safety") and [food labeling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_labeling_regulations "Food labeling regulations") concerns. Numerous countries have placed restrictions on the production, import or use of GMO foods and crops.[\[188\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-188) The [Biosafety Protocol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biosafety_Protocol "Biosafety Protocol"), an international treaty, regulates the trade of GMOs. There is ongoing discussion regarding the labeling of foods made from GMOs, and while the EU currently requires all GMO foods to be labeled, the US does not.[\[189\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-189)

Herbicide-resistant seeds have a gene implanted into their genome that allows the plants to tolerate exposure to herbicides, including [glyphosate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glyphosate "Glyphosate"). These seeds allow the farmer to grow a crop that can be sprayed with herbicides to control weeds without harming the resistant crop. Herbicide-tolerant crops are used by farmers worldwide.[\[190\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-190) With the increasing use of herbicide-tolerant crops, comes an increase in the use of glyphosate-based herbicide sprays. In some areas glyphosate resistant weeds have developed, causing farmers to switch to other herbicides.[\[191\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Farmers_Guide_to_GMOs-191)[\[192\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-192) Some studies also link widespread glyphosate usage to iron deficiencies in some crops, which is both a crop production and a nutritional quality concern, with potential economic and health implications.[\[193\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-193)

Other GMO crops used by growers include insect-resistant crops, which have a gene from the soil bacterium *[Bacillus thuringiensis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bacillus_thuringiensis "Bacillus thuringiensis")* (Bt), which produces a toxin specific to insects. These crops resist damage by insects.[\[194\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-194) Some believe that similar or better pest-resistance traits can be acquired through traditional breeding practices, and resistance to various pests can be gained through hybridization or cross-pollination with wild species. In some cases, wild species are the primary source of resistance traits; some tomato cultivars that have gained resistance to at least 19 diseases did so through crossing with wild populations of tomatoes.[\[195\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-195)

## Environmental impact

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=21 "Edit section: Environmental impact")]

Main article: [Environmental issues with agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_issues_with_agriculture "Environmental issues with agriculture")

### Effects and costs

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=22 "Edit section: Effects and costs")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/d/da/Water_pollution_in_the_Wairarapa.JPG/170px-Water_pollution_in_the_Wairarapa.JPG)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Water_pollution_in_the_Wairarapa.JPG)

[Water pollution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_pollution "Water pollution") in a rural stream due to [runoff from farming activity in New Zealand](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_pollution_in_New_Zealand "Agricultural pollution in New Zealand")

Agriculture is both a cause of and sensitive to [environmental degradation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_degradation "Environmental degradation"), such as [biodiversity loss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity_loss "Biodiversity loss"), [desertification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desertification "Desertification"), [soil degradation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soil_degradation "Soil degradation") and [climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Effects_of_climate_change_on_agriculture "Effects of climate change on agriculture"), which cause decreases in crop yield.[\[196\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-196) Agriculture is one of the most important drivers of environmental pressures, particularly habitat change, climate change, water use and toxic emissions. Agriculture is the main source of toxins released into the environment, including insecticides, especially those used on cotton.[\[197\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-197)[\[198\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-198)\[*[page needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citing_sources "Wikipedia:Citing sources")*] The 2011 UNEP Green Economy report stated that agricultural operations produced some 13 percent of anthropogenic global greenhouse gas emissions. This includes gases from the use of inorganic fertilizers, agro-chemical pesticides, and herbicides, as well as fossil fuel-energy inputs.[\[199\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-unep.org-199)

Agriculture imposes multiple external costs upon society through effects such as pesticide damage to nature (especially herbicides and insecticides), nutrient runoff, excessive water usage, and loss of natural environment. A 2000 assessment of agriculture in the UK determined total external costs for 1996 of £2,343 million, or £208 per hectare.[\[200\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Pretty2000-200) A 2005 analysis of these costs in the US concluded that cropland imposes approximately $5 to $16 billion ($30 to $96 per hectare), while livestock production imposes $714 million.[\[201\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Tegtmeier2005-201) Both studies, which focused solely on the fiscal impacts, concluded that more should be done to internalize external costs. Neither included subsidies in their analysis, but they noted that subsidies also influence the cost of agriculture to society.[\[200\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Pretty2000-200)[\[201\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Tegtmeier2005-201)

Agriculture seeks to increase yield and to reduce costs, often employing measures that cut biodiversity to very low levels. Yield increases with inputs such as fertilizers and removal of pathogens, predators, and competitors (such as weeds). Costs decrease with increasing scale of farm units, such as making fields larger; this means removing [hedges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hedge "Hedge"), ditches and other areas of habitat. Pesticides kill insects, plants and fungi. Effective yields fall with on-farm losses, which may be caused by poor production practices during harvesting, handling, and storage.[\[202\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-202)

The environmental effects of climate change show that research on pests and diseases that do not generally afflict areas is essential. In 2021, farmers discovered [stem rust](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stem_rust "Stem rust") on wheat in the [Champagne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Champagne_\(province\) "Champagne (province)") area of France, a disease that had previously only occurred in [Morocco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morocco "Morocco") for 20 to 30 years. Because of climate change, insects that used to die off over the winter are now alive and multiplying.[\[203\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-203)[\[204\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-204)

### Livestock issues

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=23 "Edit section: Livestock issues")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/f3/Biogas.jpg/220px-Biogas.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Biogas.jpg)

Farmyard [anaerobic digester](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anaerobic_digester "Anaerobic digester") converts waste plant material and manure from livestock into [biogas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biogas "Biogas") fuel.

A senior UN official, Henning Steinfeld, said that "Livestock are one of the most significant contributors to today's most serious environmental problems".[\[205\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-205) Livestock production occupies 70% of all land used for agriculture, or 30% of the land surface of the planet. It is one of the largest sources of [greenhouse gases](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenhouse_gas "Greenhouse gas"), responsible for 18% of the world's [greenhouse gas emissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenhouse_gas_emissions "Greenhouse gas emissions") as measured in CO2 equivalents. By comparison, all transportation emits 13.5% of the CO2. It produces 65% of human-related [nitrous oxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nitrous_oxide "Nitrous oxide") (which has 296 times the [global warming potential](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_warming_potential "Global warming potential") of CO2) and 37% of all human-induced [methane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methane "Methane") (which is 23 times as warming as CO2.) It also generates 64% of the [ammonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ammonia "Ammonia") emission. Livestock expansion is cited as a key factor driving [deforestation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation "Deforestation"); in the Amazon basin 70% of [previously forested area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deforestation_of_the_Amazon_Rainforest "Deforestation of the Amazon Rainforest") is now occupied by pastures and the remainder used for feed crops.[\[206\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-LEAD-206) Through deforestation and [land degradation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land_degradation "Land degradation"), livestock is also driving reductions in biodiversity. A well documented phenomenon is [woody plant encroachment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woody_plant_encroachment "Woody plant encroachment"), caused by [overgrazing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overgrazing "Overgrazing") in rangelands.[\[207\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-207) Furthermore, the [United Nations Environment Programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Environment_Programme "United Nations Environment Programme") (UNEP) states that "[methane emissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methane_emissions "Methane emissions") from global livestock are projected to increase by 60 per cent by 2030 under current practices and consumption patterns."[\[199\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-unep.org-199)

### Land and water issues

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=24 "Edit section: Land and water issues")]

See also: [Environmental impact of irrigation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_impact_of_irrigation "Environmental impact of irrigation")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/53/Share_Of_Water_Withdrawal_By_Agriculture_In_Total_Withdrawal%2C_Top_Countries_%282020%29.svg/440px-Share_Of_Water_Withdrawal_By_Agriculture_In_Total_Withdrawal%2C_Top_Countries_%282020%29.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Share_Of_Water_Withdrawal_By_Agriculture_In_Total_Withdrawal,_Top_Countries_\(2020\).svg)

Countries with the highest share of water withdrawal by agriculture in total withdrawal.

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/e/e4/Crops_Kansas_AST_20010624.jpg/240px-Crops_Kansas_AST_20010624.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Crops_Kansas_AST_20010624.jpg)

Circular [irrigated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irrigated "Irrigated") crop fields in [Kansas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haskell_County,_Kansas "Haskell County, Kansas"). Healthy, growing crops of [corn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maize "Maize") and [sorghum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sorghum "Sorghum") are green (sorghum may be slightly paler). Wheat is brilliant gold. Fields of brown have been recently harvested and plowed or have lain in [fallow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fallow "Fallow") for the year.

Land transformation, the use of land to yield goods and services, is the most substantial way humans alter the Earth's ecosystems, and is the driving force causing [biodiversity loss](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity_loss "Biodiversity loss"). Estimates of the amount of land transformed by humans vary from 39 to 50%.[\[208\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Vitousek-208) It is estimated that 24% of land globally experiences land degradation, a long-term decline in ecosystem function and productivity, with cropland being disproportionately affected.[\[209\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FAO_GLADA-209) Land management is the driving factor behind degradation; 1.5 billion people rely upon the degrading land. Degradation can be through deforestation, [desertification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desertification "Desertification"), [soil erosion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soil_erosion "Soil erosion"), mineral depletion, [acidification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soil_acidification "Soil acidification"), or [salinization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soil_salinity "Soil salinity").[\[144\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CS-144) In 2021, the global agricultural land area was 4.79 billion hectares (ha), down 2 percent, or 0.09 billion ha compared with 2000. Between 2000 and 2021, roughly two-thirds of agricultural land were used for permanent meadows and pastures (3.21 billion ha in 2021), which declined by 5 percent (0.17 billion ha). One-third of the total agricultural land was cropland (1.58 billion ha in 2021), which increased by 6 percent (0.09 billion ha).[\[108\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:14-108)

[Eutrophication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eutrophication "Eutrophication"), excessive nutrient enrichment in [aquatic ecosystems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aquatic_ecosystem "Aquatic ecosystem") resulting in [algal blooms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Algal_bloom "Algal bloom") and [anoxia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anoxic_waters "Anoxic waters"), leads to [fish kills](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fish_kill "Fish kill"), [loss of biodiversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loss_of_biodiversity "Loss of biodiversity"), and renders water unfit for drinking and other industrial uses. Excessive fertilization and manure application to cropland, as well as high livestock stocking densities cause nutrient (mainly [nitrogen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nitrogen "Nitrogen") and [phosphorus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phosphorus "Phosphorus")) [runoff](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surface_runoff "Surface runoff") and [leaching](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leaching_\(agriculture\) "Leaching (agriculture)") from agricultural land. These nutrients are major [nonpoint pollutants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nonpoint_source_pollution "Nonpoint source pollution") contributing to [eutrophication](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eutrophication "Eutrophication") of aquatic ecosystems and pollution of groundwater, with harmful effects on human populations.[\[210\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Eutr-210) Fertilizers also reduce terrestrial biodiversity by increasing competition for light, favoring those species that are able to benefit from the added nutrients.[\[211\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Hautier_Niklaus_Hector-211)

Agriculture simultaneously is facing growing freshwater demand and precipitation anomalies (droughts, floods, and extreme rainfall and weather events) on rainfed areas fields and grazing lands.[\[162\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:9-162) Agriculture accounts for 70 percent of withdrawals of freshwater resources,[\[212\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-212)[\[213\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-213) and an estimated 41 percent of current global irrigation water use occurs at the expense of environmental flow requirements.[\[162\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:9-162) It is long known that aquifers in areas as diverse as northern China, the [Upper Ganges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganges "Ganges") and the western US are being depleted, and new research extends these problems to aquifers in Iran, Mexico and Saudi Arabia.[\[214\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-214) Increasing pressure is being placed on water resources by industry and urban areas, meaning that [water scarcity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_scarcity "Water scarcity") is increasing and agriculture is facing the challenge of producing more food for the world's growing population with reduced water resources.[\[215\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-215) While industrial withdrawals have declined in the past few decades and municipal withdrawals have increased only marginally since 2010, agricultural withdrawals have continued to grow at an ever faster pace.[\[162\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:9-162) [Agricultural water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Farm_water "Farm water") usage can also cause major environmental problems, including the destruction of natural wetlands, the spread of water-borne diseases, and land degradation through salinization and waterlogging, when irrigation is performed incorrectly.[\[216\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-216)

### Pesticides

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=25 "Edit section: Pesticides")]

Main article: [Environmental impact of pesticides](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_impact_of_pesticides "Environmental impact of pesticides")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9e/Crop_spraying_near_St_Mary_Bourne_-_geograph.org.uk_-_392462.jpg/220px-Crop_spraying_near_St_Mary_Bourne_-_geograph.org.uk_-_392462.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Crop_spraying_near_St_Mary_Bourne_-_geograph.org.uk_-_392462.jpg)

Spraying a crop with a [pesticide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pesticide "Pesticide")

Pesticide use has increased since 1950 to 2.5 million short tons annually worldwide, yet crop loss from pests has remained relatively constant.[\[217\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Pimentel_pesticide-217) The World Health Organization estimated in 1992 that three million pesticide poisonings occur annually, causing 220,000 deaths.[\[218\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-WHO-218) Pesticides select for [pesticide resistance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pesticide_resistance "Pesticide resistance") in the pest population, leading to a condition termed the "pesticide treadmill" in which pest resistance warrants the development of a new pesticide.[\[219\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CS_Pest-219)

An alternative argument is that the way to "save the environment" and prevent famine is by using pesticides and intensive high yield farming, a view exemplified by a quote heading the Center for Global Food Issues website: 'Growing more per acre leaves more land for nature'.[\[220\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-DAvery-220)[\[221\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-221) However, critics argue that a trade-off between the environment and a need for food is not inevitable,[\[222\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-WH-222) and that pesticides can replace [good agronomic practices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Good_agricultural_practices "Good agricultural practices") such as crop rotation.[\[219\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-CS_Pest-219) The [Push–pull agricultural pest management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Push%E2%80%93pull_agricultural_pest_management "Push–pull agricultural pest management") technique involves intercropping, using plant aromas to repel pests from crops (push) and to lure them to a place from which they can then be removed (pull).[\[223\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-PushPull-223)

### Contribution to climate change

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=26 "Edit section: Contribution to climate change")]

Main article: [Greenhouse gas emissions from agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenhouse_gas_emissions_from_agriculture "Greenhouse gas emissions from agriculture")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/14/World_Farm-gate_Greenhouse_Gas_Emissions_By_Activity.svg/220px-World_Farm-gate_Greenhouse_Gas_Emissions_By_Activity.svg.png)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:World_Farm-gate_Greenhouse_Gas_Emissions_By_Activity.svg)

World farm-gate greenhouse gas emissions by activity

Agriculture contributes towards [climate change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_change "Climate change") through [greenhouse gas emissions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenhouse_gas_emissions "Greenhouse gas emissions") and by the conversion of non-agricultural land such as [forests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest "Forest") into agricultural land.[\[224\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-224) The agriculture, forestry and land use sector contribute between 13% and 21% of global greenhouse gas emissions.[\[225\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-IPCC2022-225) Emissions of [nitrous oxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nitrous_oxide "Nitrous oxide"), [methane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Methane "Methane") make up over half of total greenhouse gas emission from agriculture.[\[226\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-FAO2020-226) [Animal husbandry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Animal_husbandry "Animal husbandry") is a major source of greenhouse gas emissions.[\[227\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-227)

Approximately 57% of global GHG emissions from the production of food are from the production of animal-based food while plant-based foods contribute 29% and the remaining 14% is for other utilizations.[\[228\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:12-228) Farmland management and [land-use change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Land-use_change "Land-use change") represented major shares of total emissions (38% and 29%, respectively), whereas rice and beef were the largest contributing plant- and animal-based commodities (12% and 25%, respectively).[\[228\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:12-228) South and Southeast Asia and South America were the largest emitters of production-based GHGs.[\[228\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:12-228)

### Sustainability

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=27 "Edit section: Sustainability")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c4/TerracesBuffers.JPG/170px-TerracesBuffers.JPG)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:TerracesBuffers.JPG)

Terraces, [conservation tillage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservation_tillage "Conservation tillage") and conservation buffers reduce [soil erosion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soil_erosion "Soil erosion") and [water pollution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_pollution "Water pollution") on this farm in Iowa.

Main article: [Sustainable agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sustainable_agriculture "Sustainable agriculture")

Current farming methods have resulted in over-stretched water resources, high levels of erosion and reduced soil fertility. There is not enough water to continue farming using current practices; therefore how water, land, and [ecosystem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecosystem "Ecosystem") resources are used to boost crop yields must be reconsidered. A solution would be to give value to ecosystems, recognizing environmental and livelihood tradeoffs, and balancing the rights of a variety of users and interests.[\[229\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-229) Inequities that result when such measures are adopted would need to be addressed, such as the reallocation of water from poor to rich, the clearing of land to make way for more productive farmland, or the preservation of a wetland system that limits fishing rights.[\[230\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-230)

Technological advancements help provide farmers with tools and resources to make farming more sustainable.[\[231\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-231) Technology permits innovations like [conservation tillage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservation_tillage "Conservation tillage"), a farming process which helps prevent land loss to erosion, reduces water pollution, and enhances [carbon sequestration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carbon_sequestration "Carbon sequestration").[\[232\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-232)

Agricultural automation can help address some of the challenges associated with climate change and thus facilitate adaptation efforts.[\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:5-160) For example, the application of digital automation technologies (e.g. in precision agriculture) can improve resource-use efficiency in conditions which are increasingly constrained for agricultural producers.[\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:5-160) Moreover, when applied to sensing and early warning, they can help address the uncertainty and unpredictability of weather conditions associated with accelerating climate change.[\[160\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:5-160)

Other potential sustainable practices include [conservation agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservation_agriculture "Conservation agriculture"), [agroforestry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agroforestry "Agroforestry"), improved [grazing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Convertible_husbandry "Convertible husbandry"), avoided grassland conversion, and [biochar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biochar "Biochar").[\[233\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-233)[\[234\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-234) Current mono-crop farming practices in the United States preclude widespread adoption of sustainable practices, such as 2–3 crop rotations that incorporate grass or hay with annual crops, unless negative emission goals such as soil carbon sequestration become policy.[\[235\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-235)

The food demand of Earth's projected population, with current climate change predictions, could be satisfied by improvement of agricultural methods, expansion of agricultural areas, and a sustainability-oriented consumer mindset.[\[236\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-236)

### Energy dependence

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=28 "Edit section: Energy dependence")]

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/f/fd/Baumwoll-Erntemaschine_auf_Feld.jpeg/220px-Baumwoll-Erntemaschine_auf_Feld.jpeg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Baumwoll-Erntemaschine_auf_Feld.jpeg)

[Mechanized agriculture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mechanised_agriculture "Mechanised agriculture"): from the first models in the 1940s, tools like a [cotton picker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cotton_picker "Cotton picker") could replace 50 farm workers, at the price of increased use of [fossil fuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuel "Fossil fuel").

Since the 1940s, agricultural productivity has increased dramatically, due largely to the increased use of energy-intensive mechanization, fertilizers and pesticides. The vast majority of this energy input comes from [fossil fuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuel "Fossil fuel") sources.[\[237\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-237) Between the 1960s and the 1980s, the Green Revolution transformed agriculture around the globe, with world grain production increasing significantly (between 70% and 390% for wheat and 60% to 150% for rice, depending on geographic area)[\[238\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-238) as [world population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_population "World population") doubled. Heavy reliance on [petrochemicals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Petrochemical "Petrochemical") has raised concerns that oil shortages could increase costs and reduce agricultural output.[\[239\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ncseonline.org-239)

Industrialized agriculture depends on [fossil fuels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fossil_fuels "Fossil fuels") in two fundamental ways: direct consumption on the farm and manufacture of inputs used on the farm. Direct consumption includes the use of lubricants and fuels to operate farm vehicles and machinery.[\[239\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ncseonline.org-239)

Indirect consumption includes the manufacture of fertilizers, pesticides, and farm machinery.[\[239\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ncseonline.org-239) In particular, the production of [nitrogen fertilizer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nitrogen_fertilizer "Nitrogen fertilizer") can account for over half of agricultural energy usage.[\[240\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-240) Together, direct and indirect consumption by US farms accounts for about 2% of the nation's energy use. Direct and indirect energy consumption by U.S. farms peaked in 1979, and has since gradually declined.[\[239\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ncseonline.org-239) [Food systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_systems "Food systems") encompass not just agriculture but off-farm processing, packaging, transporting, marketing, consumption, and disposal of food and food-related items. Agriculture accounts for less than one-fifth of food system energy use in the US.[\[241\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-ers.usda.gov-241)[\[242\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-css.snre.umich.edu-242)

### Plastic pollution

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=29 "Edit section: Plastic pollution")]

Main articles: [Plastic pollution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plastic_pollution "Plastic pollution") and [plasticulture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plasticulture "Plasticulture")

Plastic products are used extensively in agriculture, including to increase crop yields and improve the efficiency of water and agrichemical use. "Agriplastic" products include films to cover [greenhouses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greenhouse "Greenhouse") and tunnels, mulch to cover soil (e.g. to suppress weeds, [conserve water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_conservation "Water conservation"), increase soil temperature and aid fertilizer application), shade cloth, pesticide containers, seedling trays, protective mesh and irrigation tubing. The polymers most commonly used in these products are low- density polyethylene (LPDE), linear low-density polyethylene (LLDPE), polypropylene (PP) and polyvinyl chloride (PVC).[\[243\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:0-243)

The total amount of plastics used in agriculture is difficult to quantify. A 2012 study reported that almost 6.5 million tonnes per year were consumed globally while a later study estimated that global demand in 2015 was between 7.3 million and 9 million tonnes. Widespread use of plastic mulch and lack of systematic collection and management have led to the generation of large amounts of mulch residue. Weathering and degradation eventually cause the mulch to fragment. These fragments and larger pieces of plastic accumulate in soil. Mulch residue has been measured at levels of 50 to 260 kg per hectare in topsoil in areas where mulch use dates back more than 10 years, which confirms that mulching is a major source of both microplastic and macroplastic [soil contamination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soil_contamination "Soil contamination").[\[243\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:0-243)

Agricultural plastics, especially plastic films, are not easy to recycle because of high contamination levels (up to 40–50% by weight contamination by pesticides, fertilizers, soil and debris, moist vegetation, silage juice water, and UV stabilizers) and collection difficulties . Therefore, they are often buried or abandoned in fields and watercourses or burned. These disposal practices lead to soil degradation and can result in contamination of soils and leakage of [microplastics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Microplastics "Microplastics") into the marine environment as a result of precipitation run-off and tidal washing. In addition, additives in residual plastic film (such as UV and thermal stabilizers) may have deleterious effects on crop growth, soil structure, nutrient transport and salt levels. There is a risk that plastic mulch will deteriorate [soil quality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soil_quality "Soil quality"), deplete soil organic matter stocks, increase soil water repellence and emit greenhouse gases. Microplastics released through fragmentation of agricultural plastics can absorb and concentrate contaminants capable of being passed up the trophic chain.[\[243\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:0-243)

## Disciplines

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=30 "Edit section: Disciplines")]

### Agricultural economics

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=31 "Edit section: Agricultural economics")]

Main article: [Agricultural economics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_economics "Agricultural economics")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/4/46/1846_-_Anti-Corn_Law_League_Meeting.jpg/220px-1846_-_Anti-Corn_Law_League_Meeting.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:1846_-_Anti-Corn_Law_League_Meeting.jpg)

In 19th century Britain, the [protectionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protectionist "Protectionist") [Corn Laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corn_Laws "Corn Laws") led to high prices and widespread protest, such as this 1846 meeting of the [Anti-Corn Law League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Corn_Law_League "Anti-Corn Law League").[\[244\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-244)

Agricultural economics is economics as it relates to the "production, distribution and consumption of \[agricultural] goods and services".[\[245\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-245) Combining agricultural production with general theories of marketing and business as a discipline of study began in the late 1800s, and grew significantly through the 20th century.[\[246\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-246) Although the study of agricultural economics is relatively recent, major trends in agriculture have significantly affected national and international economies throughout history, ranging from [tenant farmers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tenant_farmer "Tenant farmer") and [sharecropping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharecropping "Sharecropping") in the post-[American Civil War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Civil_War "American Civil War") Southern United States[\[247\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-247) to the European [feudal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Feudal "Feudal") system of [manorialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manorialism "Manorialism").[\[248\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-248) In the United States, and elsewhere, food costs attributed to [food processing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_processing "Food processing"), distribution, and [agricultural marketing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_marketing "Agricultural marketing"), sometimes referred to as the [value chain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_value_chain "Agricultural value chain"), have risen while the costs attributed to farming have declined. This is related to the greater efficiency of farming, combined with the increased level of [value addition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Value_added "Value added") (e.g. more highly processed products) provided by the supply chain. [Market concentration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Market_concentration "Market concentration") has increased in the sector as well, and although the total effect of the increased market concentration is likely increased efficiency, the changes redistribute [economic surplus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_surplus "Economic surplus") from producers (farmers) and consumers, and may have negative implications for rural communities.[\[249\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Sexton2000-249)

National government policies, such as taxation, [subsidies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsidy "Subsidy"), tariffs and others, can significantly change the economic marketplace for agricultural products.[\[250\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-LloydCroserAnderson2013-250) Since at least the 1960s, a combination of trade restrictions, [exchange rate policies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Exchange_rate_policy "Exchange rate policy") and subsidies have affected farmers in both the developing and the developed world. In the 1980s, non-subsidized farmers in developing countries experienced adverse effects from national policies that created artificially low global prices for farm products. Between the mid-1980s and the early 2000s, several international agreements limited agricultural tariffs, subsidies and other trade restrictions.[\[251\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-251)

However, as of 2009[\[update\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit), there was still a significant amount of policy-driven distortion in global agricultural product prices. The three agricultural products with the most trade distortion were sugar, milk and rice, mainly due to taxation. Among the [oilseeds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oilseed "Oilseed"), sesame had the most taxation, but overall, feed grains and oilseeds had much lower levels of taxation than livestock products. Since the 1980s, policy-driven distortions have decreases more among livestock products than crops during the worldwide reforms in agricultural policy.[\[250\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-LloydCroserAnderson2013-250) Despite this progress, certain crops, such as cotton, still see subsidies in developed countries artificially deflating global prices, causing hardship in developing countries with non-subsidized farmers.[\[252\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-252) Unprocessed commodities such as corn, soybeans, and cattle are generally graded to indicate quality, affecting the price the producer receives. Commodities are generally reported by production quantities, such as volume, number or weight.[\[253\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-253)

### Agricultural science

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=32 "Edit section: Agricultural science")]

Main article: [Agricultural science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_science "Agricultural science")

Further information: [Agronomy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agronomy "Agronomy")

[![](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/57/Research-mapping_plant_genomes.jpg/220px-Research-mapping_plant_genomes.jpg)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Research-mapping_plant_genomes.jpg)

An [agronomist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agronomy "Agronomy") mapping a plant [genome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Genome "Genome")

[Agricultural science](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_science "Agricultural science") is a broad multidisciplinary field of [biology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biology "Biology") that encompasses the parts of exact, natural, economic and [social sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Social_science "Social science") used in the practice and understanding of agriculture. It covers topics such as agronomy, plant breeding and genetics, [plant pathology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plant_pathology "Plant pathology"), crop modeling, soil science, [entomology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Entomology "Entomology"), production techniques and improvement, study of pests and their management, and study of adverse environmental effects such as soil degradation, [waste management](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waste_management "Waste management"), and [bioremediation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bioremediation "Bioremediation").[\[254\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Bosso_2015-254)[\[255\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Boucher_2018-255)

The scientific study of agriculture began in the 18th century, when [Johann Friedrich Mayer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Johann_Friedrich_Mayer_\(agriculturist\) "Johann Friedrich Mayer (agriculturist)") conducted experiments on the use of [gypsum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gypsum "Gypsum") (hydrated [calcium sulphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Calcium_sulphate "Calcium sulphate")) as a fertilizer.[\[256\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-JB_1840-256) Research became more systematic when in 1843, [John Lawes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Lawes "John Lawes") and Henry Gilbert began a set of long-term agronomy field experiments at [Rothamsted Research Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rothamsted_Research_Station "Rothamsted Research Station") in England; some of them, such as the [Park Grass Experiment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Park_Grass_Experiment "Park Grass Experiment"), are still running.[\[257\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-257)[\[258\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-258) In America, the [Hatch Act of 1887](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hatch_Act_of_1887 "Hatch Act of 1887") provided funding for what it was the first to call "agricultural science", driven by farmers' interest in fertilizers.[\[259\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-259) In agricultural entomology, the USDA began to research biological control in 1881; it instituted its first large program in 1905, searching Europe and Japan for natural enemies of the [spongy moth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lymantria_dispar_dispar "Lymantria dispar dispar") and [brown-tail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brown-tail "Brown-tail") moth, establishing [parasitoids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parasitoid "Parasitoid") (such as solitary wasps) and predators of both pests in the US.[\[260\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Coulson_J._R._2000-260)[\[261\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-Berkeley-261)[\[262\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-262)

## Policy

\[[edit](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Agriculture\&action=edit\&section=33 "Edit section: Policy")]

Main article: [Agricultural policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_policy "Agricultural policy")

| Product       | Subsidy |
| ------------- | ------- |
| Beef and veal | 18.0    |
| Milk          | 15.3    |
| Pigs          | 7.3     |
| Poultry       | 6.5     |
| Soybeans      | 2.3     |
| Eggs          | 1.5     |
| Sheep         | 1.1     |

[Agricultural policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agricultural_policy "Agricultural policy") is the set of government decisions and actions relating to domestic agriculture and imports of foreign agricultural products. Governments usually implement agricultural policies with the goal of achieving a specific outcome in the domestic agricultural product markets. Some overarching themes include risk management and adjustment (including policies related to climate change, food safety and natural disasters), [economic stability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_stability "Economic stability") (including policies related to taxes), natural resources and [environmental sustainability](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Environmental_sustainability "Environmental sustainability") (especially [water policy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_resource_management "Water resource management")), research and development, and market access for domestic commodities (including relations with global organizations and agreements with other countries).[\[264\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-264) Agricultural policy can also touch on [food quality](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_quality "Food quality"), ensuring that the food supply is of a consistent and known quality, food security, ensuring that the food supply meets the population's needs, and [conservation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservation_biology "Conservation biology"). Policy programs can range from financial programs, such as subsidies, to encouraging producers to enroll in voluntary quality assurance programs.[\[265\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-265)

A 2021 report finds that globally, support to agricultural producers accounts for almost US$540 billion a year.[\[266\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:11-266) This amounts to 15 percent of total agricultural production value, and is heavily biased towards measures that are leading to inefficiency, as well as are unequally distributed and harmful for the environment and human health.[\[266\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-:11-266)  

There are many influences on the creation of agricultural policy, including consumers, [agribusiness](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agribusiness "Agribusiness"), trade lobbies and other groups. Agribusiness interests hold a large amount of influence over policy making, in the form of [lobbying](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lobbying "Lobbying") and [campaign contributions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Campaign_contribution "Campaign contribution"). Political action groups, including those interested in environmental issues and labor unions, also provide influence, as do lobbying organizations representing individual agricultural commodities.[\[267\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-267) The [Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_and_Agriculture_Organization_of_the_United_Nations "Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations") (FAO) leads international efforts to defeat hunger and provides a forum for the negotiation of global agricultural regulations and agreements. Samuel Jutzi, director of FAO's animal production and health division, states that lobbying by large corporations has stopped reforms that would improve human health and the environment. For example, proposals in 2010 for a voluntary code of conduct for the livestock industry that would have provided incentives for improving standards for health, and environmental regulations, such as the number of animals an area of land can support without long-term damage, were successfully defeated due to large food company pressure.[\[268\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/#cite_note-268)
